---

## VALIDATION RULES

### Rule 1: Required Tags per Shape Biography

```yaml
validation_rule_01:
  name: shape_bio_required_tags
  description: Every Shape Biography must have these tags
  required:
    - exactly_one: gea.*
    - exactly_one: deity.meso.*
    - at_least_one: gek.*
    - at_least_one: geok-t.*
    - at_least_one: CARR__*
    - at_least_one: INV__*
```

### Rule 2: Deity-Element Consistency

```yaml
validation_rule_02:
  name: deity_element_match
  description: Deity tag must match element tag
  rules:
    - if: deity.meso.shamash
      then_must_have: gea.circle
    - if: deity.meso.ishtar
      then_must_have: gea.star8
    - if: deity.meso.nanna
      then_must_have: gea.arc
    - if: deity.meso.enlil
      then_must_have: gea.triangle
    - if: deity.meso.nabu
      then_must_have: gea.square
    - if: deity.meso.tiamat
      then_must_have: gea.spiral
    - if: deity.meso.nisaba
      then_must_have: gea.hexagon
    - if: deity.meso.marduk
      then_must_have: gea.pyramid
```

### Rule 3: Carrier-Element Consistency

```yaml
validation_rule_03:
  name: carrier_element_match
  description: Carrier tag must match its element
  rules:
    - CARR__SUN_DISK requires gea.circle
    - CARR__WHEEL requires gea.circle
    - CARR__ISHTAR_STAR requires gea.star8
    - CARR__CRESCENT_MOON requires gea.arc
    # ... etc
```

### Rule 4: Artifact Tag Completeness

```yaml
validation_rule_04:
  name: artifact_completeness
  description: Every artifact must have minimum tags
  required_per_artifact:
    - art.* (category)
    - civ.meso.* or civ.mesopotamia
    - period or date
    - location (recommended)
    - museum (if known)
```

### Rule 5: Image Requirement Tags

```yaml
validation_rule_05:
  name: image_requirement_tags
  description: Every image requirement must have type
  required:
    - img.type.*
    - role specification
```

---

## TAG AUDIT QUERIES

### Query 1: Find Untagged Content

```cypher
// Pseudo-Cypher for graph query
MATCH (sb:ShapeBio)
WHERE NOT (sb)-[:TAGGED_WITH]->(:TAG_VALUE {namespace: 'gea'})
RETURN sb.id AS missing_gea_tag
```

### Query 2: Find Invalid Tag Combinations

```cypher
MATCH (sb:ShapeBio)-[:TAGGED_WITH]->(deity:TAG_VALUE {namespace: 'deity'})
MATCH (sb)-[:TAGGED_WITH]->(elem:TAG_VALUE {namespace: 'gea'})
WHERE NOT deity.expected_element = elem.tag_string
RETURN sb.id, deity.tag_string, elem.tag_string AS mismatch
```

### Query 3: Find Deprecated Tags Still In Use

```cypher
MATCH (content)-[:TAGGED_WITH]->(tag:TAG_VALUE {deprecated: true})
RETURN content.id, tag.tag_string, tag.replaced_by
```

### Query 4: Tag Coverage Report

```cypher
MATCH (sb:ShapeBio)
OPTIONAL MATCH (sb)-[:TAGGED_WITH]->(gea:TAG_VALUE {namespace: 'gea'})
OPTIONAL MATCH (sb)-[:TAGGED_WITH]->(deity:TAG_VALUE {namespace: 'deity'})
OPTIONAL MATCH (sb)-[:TAGGED_WITH]->(gek:TAG_VALUE {namespace: 'gek'})
OPTIONAL MATCH (sb)-[:TAGGED_WITH]->(geok:TAG_VALUE {namespace: 'geok-t'})
RETURN sb.id,
       CASE WHEN gea IS NOT NULL THEN '✓' ELSE '✗' END AS has_gea,
       CASE WHEN deity IS NOT NULL THEN '✓' ELSE '✗' END AS has_deity,
       CASE WHEN gek IS NOT NULL THEN '✓' ELSE '✗' END AS has_gek,
       CASE WHEN geok IS NOT NULL THEN '✓' ELSE '✗' END AS has_geok
```

---

## TAG UPDATE PROPAGATION

When a tag is updated or deprecated:

```yaml
update_protocol:
  1_mark_deprecated:
    - Set tag.deprecated = true
    - Set tag.replaced_by = new_tag_id
    - Set tag.deprecated_at = timestamp

  2_find_affected:
    - Query all TAG_INSTANCE with this tag
    - Generate report of affected content

  3_notify:
    - Log to tag_changes.log
    - Add to validation queue

  4_migrate (optional):
    - If auto_migrate = true
    - Create new TAG_INSTANCE with replacement tag
    - Mark old instance as migrated

  5_validate:
    - Run validation rules on affected content
    - Report any new violations
```

---

## SHAPE BIOGRAPHY TAG CHECKLIST

For each element in the Shape Biography, verify:

### Circle (SB_MESO_CIRCLE_V1)

| Tag Category | Required | Present | Valid |
|--------------|----------|---------|-------|
| GEA | gea.circle | ☐ | ☐ |

## IMPLEMENTATION: GRAPH NODE SCRIPT

```javascript
// tag-validation-graph.js
// Creates graph nodes for tag validation system

const TAG_NAMESPACES = [
  { id: 'ns_gea', name: 'GEA - Atomic Elements', prefix: 'gea.' },
  { id: 'ns_gem', name: 'GEM - Molecular Composites', prefix: 'gem.' },
  { id: 'ns_gek', name: 'GEK - Conceptual', prefix: 'gek.' },
  { id: 'ns_geu', name: 'GEU - Ubiquitous', prefix: 'geu.' },
  { id: 'ns_geok_t', name: 'GEOK-T - Transformations', prefix: 'geok-t.' },
  { id: 'ns_deity', name: 'Deity', prefix: 'deity.' },
  { id: 'ns_carrier', name: 'Carrier', prefix: 'CARR__' },
  { id: 'ns_invention', name: 'Invention', prefix: 'INV__' },
];

function validateShapeBio(shapeBioId, tags) {
  const errors = [];
  const warnings = [];

  // Rule 1: Required namespaces
  const requiredNamespaces = ['gea', 'deity', 'geok-t'];
  for (const ns of requiredNamespaces) {
    const hasTag = tags.some(t => t.startsWith(ns + '.') || t.startsWith(ns.toUpperCase() + '__'));
    if (!hasTag) {
      errors.push(`Missing required tag namespace: ${ns}`);
    }
  }

  // Rule 2: Deity-Element consistency
  const deityTag = tags.find(t => t.startsWith('deity.meso.'));
  const geaTag = tags.find(t => t.startsWith('gea.'));
  if (deityTag && geaTag) {
    const expectedElement = DEITY_ELEMENT_MAP[deityTag];
    if (expectedElement && expectedElement !== geaTag) {
      errors.push(`Deity ${deityTag} expects element ${expectedElement}, but found ${geaTag}`);
    }
  }

  // Rule 3: At least one carrier
  const hasCarrier = tags.some(t => t.startsWith('CARR__'));
  if (!hasCarrier) {
    warnings.push('No carrier tag found');
  }

  // Rule 4: At least one invention
  const hasInvention = tags.some(t => t.startsWith('INV__'));
  if (!hasInvention) {
    warnings.push('No invention tag found');
  }

  return { shapeBioId, valid: errors.length === 0, errors, warnings };
}

const DEITY_ELEMENT_MAP = {
  'deity.meso.shamash': 'gea.circle',
  'deity.meso.ishtar': 'gea.star8',
  'deity.meso.nanna': 'gea.arc',
  'deity.meso.enlil': 'gea.triangle',
  'deity.meso.nabu': 'gea.square',
  'deity.meso.tiamat': 'gea.spiral',
  'deity.meso.nisaba': 'gea.hexagon',
  'deity.meso.marduk': 'gea.pyramid',
};

module.exports = { validateShapeBio, TAG_NAMESPACES, DEITY_ELEMENT_MAP };
```

## UNKNOWN TAG HANDLING (NOVELTY INTAKE)

When a tag is unrecognized:
- Preserve it as-is in content
- Create/attach a `TAG_VALUE` node with `deprecated: false` and `status: unknown`
- Attach `flag.tag_unknown` and route to Novelty Intake for later taxonomy expansion
SSOT-018: COMPREHENSIVE TAG EXTRACTIONSSOT-018-COMPREHENSIVE-TAG-EXTRACTION.md 
# SSOT-018: COMPREHENSIVE TAG EXTRACTION
version: 5.0.0
generated_at: 2026-02-02

# SSOT-018: COMPREHENSIVE TAG EXTRACTION

## Purpose

This document extracts and catalogs **EVERY taggable entity** from the Shape Biography and related curriculum materials. It serves as the complete reference for what must be tagged in the graph node system.

---

## TAG NAMESPACE HIERARCHY

```
CANON_TAG_ROOT
├── GE (Geometric Elements)
│   ├── GEA (Atomic)
│   ├── GEM (Molecular)
│   ├── GEK (Conceptual/Mathematical)
│   ├── GEU (Ubiquitous/Cross-cultural)
│   └── GEOK-T (Transformations)
│
├── HIST (Historical)
│   ├── CIV (Civilizations)
│   ├── PERIOD (Time Periods)
│   ├── RULER (Kings/Rulers)
│   ├── DYNASTY (Dynasties)
│   └── EVENT (Historical Events)
│
├── GEO (Geographic)
│   ├── CITY (Cities/Sites)
│   ├── REGION (Regions)
│   ├── RIVER (Rivers)
│   └── TEMPLE (Temple Complexes)
│
├── ART (Artifacts)
│   ├── TYPE (Artifact Types)
│   ├── MATERIAL (Materials)
│   ├── TECHNIQUE (Production Techniques)
│   └── MUSEUM (Museum Collections)
│
├── INV (Inventions)
│   ├── MECH (Mechanical)
│   ├── ARCH (Architectural)
│   ├── ADMIN (Administrative)
│   └── ASTRO (Astronomical)
│
├── DEITY (Divine Figures)
│   ├── MESO (Mesopotamian)


> **MULTI-CIV NOTE:** Deity tags are scoped by civilization: `deity.<civ>.<name>`. Mesopotamia is only one civ pack example.
│   ├── EGYPT (Egyptian)
│   └── GREECE (Greek)
│
├── SCI (Scientific Concepts)
│   ├── PHYS (Physics)
│   ├── MATH (Mathematics)
│   ├── ASTRO (Astronomy)
│   └── ENG (Engineering)
│
├── IMG (Image System)
│   ├── TYPE (Image Types)
│   ├── ROLE (Image Roles)
│   ├── SRC (Sources)
│   └── STATUS (Production Status)
│
└── CURR (Curriculum)
    ├── SSOT (Section Codes)
    ├── GRADE (Grade Levels)
    ├── WEEK (Week Numbers)
    └── DAY (Day A/B)
```

## CORE PRINCIPLES

### The Three Foundational Truths

1. **Metaphor Geometry Function: A Superimposed State where Metaphor and Function to co-exist**
   - Metaphor refers to symbolic thought processes that defines through association.  Metaphor enables understanding and problem solving.  
   - Function refers to individual geometric elements mathematical properties, scientific properties  
   - There is no causality necessary.  Geometry is subject agnostic.  
   - Metaphor is not exactly equivalent to, it does not merely "relate to" or "connects to". 
   - The SAME geometric property creates both meaning and capability
   - Example: Equal radius IS fairness (metaphor) AND IS fair distribution (function)

2. **Content Separate from Pedagogy**
   - WHAT we teach ≠ HOW we teach
   - Content = Geometric properties, artifacts, provable claims
   - Pedagogy = Methods, activities, engagement strategies

3. **Evidence Before Conclusions**
   - You cannot claim something without artifacts to prove it
   - Every statement must be backed by specific, citable content
   - Propositional logic: p → q iff w (Evidence → Claim only if Warrant exists)

---

## WHAT IS AN SSOT?

**SSOT = Single Source of Truth**

An SSOT is a document that defines ONE aspect of the curriculum system with complete authority. When there's a conflict or question, the SSOT is the final answer.

### Why SSOTs?

**The Problem:**
- Multiple documents saying different things
- Contradictions between files
- Unclear which version is correct
- Hours wasted finding "the right answer"

**The Solution:**
- ONE document per topic
- That document SUPERSEDES all others
- Clear hierarchy of which SSOT trumps which
- Always know where to look

### SSOT vs. Regular Document

| Regular Document | SSOT Document |
|------------------|---------------|
| Can be superseded | Cannot be superseded (except by higher SSOT) |
| May contradict others | Defines the truth |
| Reference material | Authoritative source |
| "One way to do it" | "THE way to do it" |

---

## HOW TO READ SSOTs

### SSOT Document Structure

Most SSOTs follow this pattern:

```markdown
# TITLE
## Subtitle / Version Info

## ⚠️ NOTICE
(What this supersedes, what's critical)

## WHAT THIS IS
(Definition and purpose)

## THE RULES / REQUIREMENTS
(Non-negotiable specifications)

## EXAMPLES
(Correct and incorrect examples)

## FORMAT / TEMPLATE
(If applicable: exact formats to use)

## QUALITY CHECKS
(How to validate compliance)

## END OF SSOT
```

### How to Use an SSOT

1. **Read the ⚠️ NOTICE first**
   - Tells you what this supersedes
   - Alerts you to critical updates

2. **Read "WHAT THIS IS"**
   - Understand the purpose
   - Know when to use this SSOT

3. **Read "THE RULES"**
   - These are non-negotiable
   - Cannot be modified without system-wide update

4. **Study the EXAMPLES**
   - See correct application
   - Learn from violations

5. **Use the FORMAT**
   - Copy templates exactly
   - Adapt only where explicitly allowed

6. **Run QUALITY CHECKS**
   - Validate before finalizing
   - Ensure compliance

## CRITICAL TERMINOLOGY

### Terms You MUST Know

#### **CAR (Content Acquisition Restatement)**
**Replaces:** "Learning Objective" or "LO"

**Definition:** A declarative statement that restates what acquired content (artifacts, myths, observations) proves through propositional logic.

**NOT:** "Students will understand..."
**YES:** "Shamash's circular path proves fairness because constant radius creates equal treatment."

**Full documentation:** CONTENT_ACQUISITION_RESTATEMENT_SSOT.md

---

#### **Propositional Logic (p → q iff w)**
**What it means:**
```
p = Evidence (artifact, observation)
q = Claim (what we're stating)
w = Warrant (geometric property connecting p to q)
```

**Translation:** "Evidence demonstrates Claim if and only if Warrant exists."

**Example:**
- p: Shamash's circular path (from cylinder seal BM 89115)
- q: Represents fairness
- w: Circles maintain equal radius = equal treatment

---

#### **Metaphor = Function (NOT "relates to")**
**Wrong:** "Circles relate to fairness in meaning and structure."
**Right:** "Equal radius IS fairness—the SAME property creates both meaning and capability."

**The Thesis:** The metaphoric meaning and functional property are THE SAME THING, not two related things.

---

#### **Pedagogical vs. Content**
**Pedagogical:** HOW we teach (methods, activities, engagement)
**Content:** WHAT we teach (geometric properties, artifacts, conclusions)

**Must be separate:** Never mix "how" and "what" in the same field/statement.

---

#### **Artifact Description vs. Teaching Question**
**Artifact Description (Evidence):** MUST be declarative, identify features
- "Cylinder seal BM 89115: Shamash with seven radial beams, each 2.3cm from shoulders"

**Teaching Question (Pedagogy):** CAN be interrogative
- "Now that we see equal rays, why might that create fairness?"

**NEVER confuse these** - Evidence statements can't ask questions.

---

#### **The Six Non-Negotiables**
The six requirements every CAR MUST satisfy (see full section below).

---

## THE SIX NON-NEGOTIABLE GUARDRAILS

Every Content Acquisition Restatement (CAR) MUST pass all six:

### 1. Evidence Must Exist
❌ "Circles represent fairness"
✅ "Shamash's circular path [cylinder seal BM 89115] represents fairness"

**The test:** Can you point to the specific artifact?

---

### 2. Warrant Must Be Geometric
❌ "Shamash shows fairness because he is powerful"
✅ "Shamash shows fairness because circles maintain equal radius"

**The test:** Is the "because" clause a geometric property?

---

### 3. Logic Must Chain
❌ Section A3 CAR unrelated to Section A2
✅ Section A3 uses A2's conclusion + warrant as premise

**The test:** Does this section build on the previous one?

---

### 4. CARs Are Declarative, Not Activity Goals
❌ "Students will learn about circles"
✅ "Circles demonstrate fairness because equal radius creates equal treatment"

**The test:** Does it state what content proves, not what students do?

---

### 5. Artifacts Must Be Real and Cited
❌ "Ancient Mesopotamians used circles in rituals"
✅ "Cylinder seal BM 89115 shows Shamash with radial beams"

**The test:** Is there museum provenance and specific identification?

---

### 6. Artifact Descriptions IDENTIFY Features
❌ "Look at this seal. What do you notice?"
✅ "Seal shows seven radial beams, each 2.3cm from center, evenly spaced"

**The test:** Does the artifact caption state facts (not ask questions)?

## HOW TO UPDATE THE SYSTEM

### When You Need to Change Something

#### **Step 1: Identify the SSOT**

Ask: "What am I changing?"

| What You're Changing | Which SSOT | Location |
|---------------------|-----------|----------|
| Section structure/rules | MASTER_SSOT | KEEP/SSOT_SYSTEM_V8/ |
| CAR format/requirements | CAR SSOT | UI-SYS_MANIFESTS AND GUARDRAILS/ |
| Element definitions | SSOT-004 | Backend/ssot-ontologies.js |
| Deity definitions | SSOT-005 | Backend/ssot-ontologies.js |
| Artifact categories | SSOT-006 | Backend/ssot-ontologies.js |
| Manifest process | build-manifest.md | UI-SYS_MANIFESTS AND GUARDRAILS/ |

---

#### **Step 2: Check SSOT Hierarchy**

**Can you make this change?**

✅ **YES if:**
- You're updating an example (doesn't change rules)
- You're clarifying language (doesn't change requirements)
- You're adding new elements/deities (following existing pattern)

❌ **NO (requires system-wide update) if:**
- Changing the six non-negotiables
- Modifying section structure (16 sections)
- Changing propositional logic framework
- Altering Metaphor = Function thesis

---

#### **Step 3: Make the Change**

1. **Update the SSOT document**
2. **Update the "Last Modified" date**
3. **Add a note to the ⚠️ NOTICE section if major change**
4. **Check for dependent SSOTs** that reference this one
5. **Update dependent SSOTs** to reflect change
6. **Update this Constitution** if it's a major change

---

#### **Step 4: Validate the Change**

Run through:
- [ ] Does this contradict any higher SSOT?
- [ ] Have I updated all dependent documents?
- [ ] Have I tested with an example?
- [ ] Does this maintain the three core principles?
- [ ] Does this preserve the six non-negotiables?

---

### Example: Adding a New Geometric Element

**Scenario:** You want to add "Rhombus" as a new element.

**Process:**

1. **Identify SSOT:** SSOT-004 (Geometric Elements) in `ssot-ontologies.js`

2. **Check hierarchy:** SSOT-004 is Level 2, can add elements if following pattern

3. **Make changes:**
   ```javascript
   // In ssot-ontologies.js
   'elem.rhombus': {
     id: 'elem.rhombus',
     name: 'Rhombus',
     core_property: 'Equal sides with variable angles',
     metaphor_potential: 'Flexibility within structure',
     function_potential: 'Tessellation and tiling',
     deity_pairing: ['deity.nabu'], // If appropriate
     grade_appropriate: [4, 5]
   }
   ```

4. **Update dependent docs:**
   - Update MASTER_SSOT element count (if it lists them)
   - Add to element sequence if needed
   - Create week structure if it gets one

5. **Validate:**
   - [ ] Follows same structure as other elements
   - [ ] Has core property defined
   - [ ] Has metaphor + function pairing
   - [ ] Deity pairing makes sense (or is left null)

## WORKING WITH THE USER INTERFACE

### The Curriculum Studio UI

The UI is built on these SSOTs. When you see something in the UI, it's enforcing an SSOT rule.

#### **UI Components → SSOT Mapping**

| UI Component | Enforces Which SSOT | What It Does |
|--------------|-------------------|--------------|
| **Section Editor** | MASTER_SSOT (002) | Enforces 16 sections, order, required fields |
| **CAR Input Field** | CAR SSOT | Validates propositional logic, checks evidence |
| **Artifact Selector** | SSOT-006 | Ensures artifact categories are correct |
| **A4 Subsection Builder** | SSOT-006 + A4 Guardrail | Validates 2+ subsections, different categories |
| **Element/Deity Pairing** | SSOT-004 + SSOT-005 | Only allows valid pairings |
| **Bridge Section Validator** | Metaphor = Function SSOT | Checks for "IS" language |

---

### Common UI Validation Messages

| Message | What It Means | Which SSOT | How to Fix |
|---------|--------------|-----------|------------|
| "CAR must cite evidence" | No artifact in statement | CAR SSOT (#1) | Add artifact reference |
| "Warrant must be geometric" | "Because" clause not geometric | CAR SSOT (#2) | Use geometric property |
| "Use 'IS' language in bridge" | Says "relates to" not "is" | Metaphor = Function | Change to "IS" |
| "A4 needs 2+ subsections" | Only 1 artifact category | SSOT-006 A4 Guardrail | Add different category |
| "Artifact description asks question" | Caption interrogative | CAR SSOT (#6) | Make declarative |
| "Section doesn't chain" | Doesn't use previous output | CAR SSOT (#3) | Use A2 conclusion in A3 |

---

### Where to Find Things in the UI

#### **Creating a Manifest:**
1. Select Grade (3, 4, or 5)
2. Select Week (I-VIII)
3. System auto-populates:
   - Element (from SSOT-004)
   - Deity (from SSOT-005, matched to element)
   - Week thesis template (from MASTER_SSOT)

#### **Writing a Section:**
1. Select Section Code (A1-A8, B1-B8)
2. System shows:
   - Section requirements (from MASTER_SSOT)
   - CAR template (from CAR SSOT)
   - Required artifacts (from SSOT-006)

3. You provide:
   - Content Acquisition Restatement (CAR)
   - Pedagogical Design (separate field)
   - Content Purpose (separate field)
   - Artifact IDs (validated against SSOT-006)

#### **Validating Your Work:**
- Green check = Passes all guardrails
- Yellow warning = Minor issue (can proceed)
- Red X = Violates SSOT (must fix)

Click the warning/error to see:
- Which SSOT is violated
- Which guardrail specifically
- How to fix it

## COMMON TASKS & WHERE TO GO

### "I need to write a CAR for Section A4"

**Go to:**
1. CAR SSOT (for format)
2. MASTER_SSOT Section A4 definition (for requirements)
3. SSOT-006 (for artifact types A4a-A4f)

**Steps:**
1. Read A4 requirements (2+ subsections, different categories)
2. Check CAR format (Evidence → Claim iff Warrant)
3. Cite specific artifacts (not plural "seals")
4. Ensure artifact descriptions identify features
5. Write CAR following propositional logic

---

### "I'm building a manifest for Grade 3 Week II"

**Go to:**
1. build-manifest.md (overall process)
2. ARCHIVE/MANIFEST GRADE 3.md (gold standard example)
3. MASTER_SSOT (section structure)

**Steps:**
1. Follow 7-step process in build-manifest.md
2. Use Grade 3 Week I as template
3. Validate against quality checklist
4. Count artifacts at end

---

### "I need to understand the Metaphor = Function thesis"

**Go to:**
1. CORE_THESIS_METAPHOR_EQUALS_FUNCTION.md
2. MASTER_SSOT (Bridge section B1)

**Key points:**
- Use "IS" not "relates to"
- Same property in both domains
- Bridge section must prove identity

---

### "I need to add a new artifact type"

**Go to:**
1. SSOT-006 (in backend ssot-ontologies.js)
2. Check if it fits existing 6 categories
3. If not, this is a MAJOR change → requires system update

**Process:**
- If it fits existing (e.g., new Sacred artifact) → just add to database
- If new category (e.g., "Agricultural") → update SSOT-006, MASTER_SSOT, all manifests

---

### "Something's broken/contradictory"

**Go to:**
1. This Constitution
2. Check SSOT hierarchy
3. Find which SSOT supersedes

**Process:**
1. Identify the contradiction
2. Find which SSOTs are involved
3. Higher SSOT wins (MASTER > Specialized > Operational)
4. Update lower SSOT to match
5. If both same level → check dates (newer wins, unless noted)

---

### "I want to change terminology"

**Example:** Like changing "Learning Objectives" to "Content Acquisition Restatements"

**Go to:**
1. Identify scope (how many docs affected?)
2. Update highest-level SSOT first (MASTER_SSOT)
3. Update specialized SSOTs
4. Update operational docs
5. Update UI
6. Update database field names
7. Update API endpoints
8. **Update this Constitution**

**This is a MAJOR change** - expect to touch 20+ files.

## QUICK REFERENCE CARDS

### The Three Core Principles

```
1. Metaphor IS Function (same property, both domains)
2. Content ≠ Pedagogy (separate what from how)
3. Evidence Before Conclusions (artifacts → claims)
```

### The Six Non-Negotiables

```
1. Evidence must exist (cite artifacts)
2. Warrant must be geometric (geometric property)
3. Logic must chain (section n+1 uses section n)
4. CARs are declarative (not "Students will...")
5. Artifacts real and cited (museum provenance)
6. Artifact descriptions identify (not ask questions)
```

### The Propositional Logic Formula

```
p → q iff w

p = Evidence
q = Claim
w = Warrant

"Evidence proves Claim if and only if Warrant exists"
```

### The SSOT Hierarchy

```
Level 1: MASTER_SSOT_SUPERSEDES_ALL.md
Level 2: SSOT-002, SSOT-004, SSOT-005, SSOT-006
Level 3: CAR SSOT, Build Manifest SSOT
Level 4: Guardrail documents
```

---

## WHEN IN DOUBT

### The Decision Tree

```
START: Do I need to change/create something?
│
├─ Is it changing structure/rules?
│  └─ YES → Check MASTER_SSOT first
│      └─ Violates SSOT? → DON'T DO IT
│      └─ Allowed? → Proceed, update dependents
│
├─ Is it a CAR/section conclusion?
│  └─ YES → Follow CAR SSOT six non-negotiables
│      └─ Passes all six? → Approved
│      └─ Fails any? → Fix until passes
│
├─ Is it artifact-related?
│  └─ YES → Check SSOT-006 categories
│      └─ Fits existing? → Add to database
│      └─ New category? → Major change, update system
│
└─ Is it contradictory to existing?
   └─ YES → Find SSOTs involved
       └─ Higher SSOT wins → Update lower
```

**This system exists to prevent the problems we've experienced:**

- ❌ Hours searching for "the right version"
- ❌ Contradictions between documents
- ❌ Unclear which source is authoritative
- ❌ Wasted time on rework

**The SSOTs solve this by:**

- ✅ ONE source of truth per topic
- ✅ Clear hierarchy when conflicts arise
- ✅ Explicit supersession notices
- ✅ Validation at every step

**When you follow the SSOTs, you:**
- Know your work is correct
- Know it won't contradict later
- Know where to look when confused
- Save hours of debugging

**This Constitution helps you:**
- Understand what SSOTs are
- Know which one to use when
- Make changes correctly
- Keep the system consistent

To build a system with these specific capabilities, you need a modular **microservices architecture** that separates the "Research Brain" (Back-end AI) from the "Creation Sandbox" (Front-end Tools)./Users/samimajeed/Documents/Obsidian - Main Vault/PILOT - Mesapotamia Curriculum/00_SYSTEM FILES_1.26/AUDIT - CURRENT FUNCTIONALITY/PRODUCTION LINE SYSTEM TO CLEAN UP/FRMR-SYS/zUI-SYS_/UI-V2-CONTENT PRODUCTION/MICROSERVICES ARCHITECTURE.md 
To build a system with these specific capabilities, you need a modular **microservices architecture** that separates the "Research Brain" (Back-end AI) from the "Creation Sandbox" (Front-end Tools).

The following is a breakdown of every technical component required, with a specific focus on the automated image pairing engine you requested.

**1. The "Brain": Research & Curriculum Generation**

This module autonomously gathers information, structures it, and writes the curriculum.

- **Orchestration Agent:** **LangChain** or **AutoGPT** framework to manage the multi-step research process.
- **Research Sources (Ingestion):**
    - **SerpApi** or **Google Custom Search JSON API**: For real-time web scraping of current topics.
    - **Common Crawl / Wikipedia Dumps**: For foundational static knowledge.
    - **PDF Parsers (PyPDF2 / unstructured.io)**: To ingest academic papers/textbooks.
- **Subject Integration (Graph Modeling):**
    - **Graph Database:** **Neo4j** is the industry standard here. Use it to store concepts as "Nodes" and relationships (e.g., "Prerequisite for", "Part of", "Historical Context of") as "Edges".
    - **Graph Builder:** **Neo4j LLM Graph Builder**. This specific tool uses an LLM to read unstructured text and automatically convert it into a structured Knowledge Graph (Cypher queries).
    - **Vector Database:** **Pinecone** or **Milvus**. You must store "embeddings" (numerical representations) of every paragraph generated to allow for semantic searching later.
- **Taxonomy & Tagging:**
    - **PoolParty Semantic Suite** (Enterprise) or custom **SKOS** (Simple Knowledge Organization System) taxonomies generated by an LLM (e.g., Claude 3.5 Sonnet) to automatically tag content with hierarchical labels (Subject > Topic > Sub-topic).

**2. The "Sandbox": Creation & Editing Tools**

You cannot build this effectively from scratch; you must integrate specific open-source JavaScript libraries to create the "Google Docs/Canva" experience in the browser.

- **Document Editor (Word Processor):**
    - **Framework:** **Prosemirror** or **Tiptap** (Headless wrapper for Prosemirror). These provide the rich-text engine.
    - **Collaboration:** **Y.js**. This is _critical_ for real-time collaboration (like Google Docs). It handles "Conflict-free Replicated Data Types" (CRDTs) so multiple users can edit without overwriting each other.
- **Photo Editor (Canva-lite):**
    - **Canvas Engine:** **Fabric.js** or **Konva.js**. These libraries allow you to draw images, text, and shapes on an HTML5 canvas and manipulate them (rotate, resize, layers).
    - **Image Processing:** **Filerobot Image Editor** or **Toast UI Image Editor**. These are pre-built React components that give you crop, filter, and adjustment UI out of the box.
- **Presentation Builder:**
    - **Web Framework:** **Reveal.js** (HTML-based slides) or **Spectacle** (React-based slides).
    - **File Generation:** **PptxGenJS**. This library allows the browser to take the user's design and export it as a genuine Microsoft PowerPoint (.pptx) file.
- **Website Builder:**
    - **Engine:** **GrapesJS**. This is the leading open-source "drag-and-drop" web builder framework. It handles the HTML/CSS generation and template management.

**3. "Finding & Pairing Images": The Deep Dive**

To automate the pairing of research content with relevant images without human intervention, you need a **Semantic Image Retrieval Pipeline**.

**The Component Stack:**

1. **Image Source APIs:**
    - **Unsplash API** (High quality, artistic).
    - **Pexels API** (Video & Photo).
    - **Creative Commons Catalog API** (Openverse) for open-license educational content.
2. **The Matching Brain: OpenAI CLIP (Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training):**
    - **How it works:** CLIP is a neural network trained to understand text and images in the same "vector space."
    - **The Process:**
        1. **Embedding:** When your AI writes a paragraph (e.g., "The mitochondria is the powerhouse of the cell..."), you send that text to the CLIP text encoder to get a vector.
        2. **Retrieval:** You fetch 50-100 candidate images from Unsplash using basic keywords (extracted via NLP).
        3. **Scoring:** You run those candidate images through the CLIP image encoder.
        4. **Ranking:** You calculate the **Cosine Similarity** between the _Text Vector_ and the _Image Vectors_. The image with the highest score is mathematically the best semantic match for that specific paragraph.
3. **Integrity Check:**
    - **Google Cloud Vision API** (SafeSearch detection) to automatically filter out inappropriate content before it reaches the teacher.

**4. The Teacher Portal (Delivery End)**

- **Headless CMS:** **Strapi** or **Contentstack**. This stores all the "finalized" bundles (text + images + videos) separate from the display logic.
- **LMS Front-end:** Built in **Next.js** or **React**.
- **Interactive Features:**
    - **H5P**: The standard for HTML5 interactive content. You can embed H5P content types (flashcards, quizzes, interactive videos) directly into your generated pages.
- **AI Video Generation:**
    - **HeyGen API** or **D-ID API**: To generate "Talking Head" avatars reading the scripts your research AI wrote.
    - **Shotstack API**: To programmatically edit video clips, overlays, and text together into a final MP4 file.

**Summary Architecture Diagram**

|Feature|Recommended Component / Library|
|---|---|
|**Research Logic**|Python + LangChain + Scrapy|
|**Graph Database**|Neo4j + Neo4j LLM Builder|
|**Image Matching**|**OpenAI CLIP** + Unsplash API|
|**Collab Editing**|**Y.js** + Tiptap (Text) / Fabric.js (Image)|
|**Slide Export**|PptxGenJS|
|**Web Builder**|GrapesJS|
|**Video Gen**|HeyGen API + Shotstack|
|**Database**|PostgreSQL (User data) + Pinecone (Vectors)|

Here is how you structure a component that combines **R3F (3D)** with **Rive (Character)** to create that friendly "Kinship" feel.

To build a desktop OS app (via Electron) that feels like a **living, breathing world** rather than a "blueprint" or textbook, you need a stack that prioritizes **Game Feel** (often called "Juice") over standard web design.

For children, "kinship" comes from **responsiveness** and **personality**. The interface should bounce, react, and have a "face."

Here is the **"Living Classroom" Stack** designed specifically for high-fidelity animation and physics on a desktop app.

### 1. The Core Architecture (The "One Engine")

Don't build separate "Art" and "STEM" renderers. Use a single 3D environment for everything.

- **Framework:** **Electron + React + TypeScript**
    
    - _Why:_ Desktop performance with web flexibility.
        
- **Visual Engine:** **React Three Fiber (R3F)** (Three.js for React)
    
    - _Why for STEM:_ It renders the 3D wheel/lever/pulley.
        
    - _Why for ART:_ Instead of a flat `<img>` tag for the "Ishtar Gate," you render it as a **Texture on a 3D Plane**. This allows you to simulate "sunlight" hitting the relief carvings (normal maps) or tilt the painting to see brush strokes. It unifies Art and Science into one physical world.
        

### 2. The "Kinship" Layer (The Secret Weapon)

To avoid the "blueprint" look, you need a **Guide**—an animated character that lives in the UI, not just a static helper text.

- **Character Animation:** **Rive** (via `rive-react`)
    
    - _What it is:_ Interactive vector animation. Unlike a video or GIF, a Rive character can "look" at where the user's mouse is, "cheer" when they solve a puzzle, or look "confused" if the physics fails.
        
    - _The "Kinship" Effect:_ If the child drags the slider too fast, the character can grab the side of the screen and hold on. This builds an emotional connection.
        

### 3. The "Truth" Layer (Physics & Logic)

- **Physics Engine:** **Rapier** (via `@react-three/rapier`)
    
    - _Why:_ It’s the fastest physics engine for the web (WASM-based). It handles the "sliding" friction and "balancing" torque instantly.
        
    - _Child-Friendly Feature:_ It’s deterministic. If they build a stable arch, it stays up. If they pull the keystone, it collapses realistically.
        
- **UI Animation:** **Framer Motion**
    
    - _Why:_ Standard CSS transitions feel stiff. Framer Motion gives you "spring" physics for buttons and menus. When a child opens the "Tool Palette," it shouldn't just _appear_; it should _bounce_ onto the screen like a toy falling out of a box.
        

---

### How this Stack Comes Together (The "Lever" Example)

Imagine the **Lesson: "The Lever"**.

1. **The Visuals (R3F):** The screen shows a 3D garden. A heavy rock (3D mesh) is blocking the path.
    
2. **The Guide (Rive):** A small animated Gilgamesh character points at the rock and struggles to push it (animation loop: `push_fail`).
    
3. **The Interaction (Rapier):** The child drags a "Plank" and a "Fulcrum" from the inventory. They physically collide with the ground.
    
4. **The "Kinship" (Logic):** As the child moves the fulcrum closer to the rock, the Gilgamesh character smiles and gives a thumbs up (state change: `happy`).
    
5. **The Success:** The child pushes the lever. Rapier calculates the torque. The rock rolls away. Gilgamesh does a victory dance.
    

### Implementation: The "Living World" Component

Here is how you structure a component that combines **R3F (3D)** with **Rive (Character)** to create that friendly "Kinship" feel.

TypeScript

```
import React, { useState, useEffect } from 'react';
import { Canvas } from '@react-three/fiber';
import { Physics, RigidBody } from '@react-three/rapier';
import { useRive, useStateMachineInput } from '@rive-app/react-canvas';
import { motion } from 'framer-motion';

// 1. THE CHARACTER (The "Face" of the App)
const Assistant = ({ mood }) => {
  const { rive, RiveComponent } = useRive({
    src: 'assistant_character.riv',
    stateMachines: 'State Machine 1',
    autoplay: true,
  });

  // Connect React state to Rive animation inputs
  const moodInput = useStateMachineInput(rive, 'State Machine 1', 'MoodLevel');
  
  useEffect(() => {
    if (moodInput) moodInput.value = mood; // 0 = Sad, 100 = Happy
  }, [mood, moodInput]);

  return <RiveComponent style={{ width: '150px', height: '150px' }} />;
};

// 2. THE 3D OBJECT (The Lesson)
const InteractiveLever = ({ onSuccess }) => {
  // Use Rapier for real physics interaction
  return (
    <RigidBody colliders="hull" onCollisionEnter={onSuccess}>
      <mesh castShadow receiveShadow>
        <boxGeometry args={[5, 0.5, 1]} /> {/* The Plank */}
        <meshStandardMaterial color="#8B4513" />
      </mesh>
    </RigidBody>
  );
};

// 3. THE MAIN "LIVING" PAGE
export default function LivingLesson() {
  const [success, setSuccess] = useState(false);

  return (
    <div className="h-screen w-screen bg-blue-50 relative overflow-hidden">
      
      {/* BACKGROUND: 3D World (The Stage) */}
      <div className="absolute inset-0 z-0">
        <Canvas shadows camera={{ position: [0, 5, 10], fov: 50 }}>
          <ambientLight intensity={0.5} />
          <directionalLight position={[10, 10, 5]} castShadow />
          
          <Physics gravity={[0, -9.81, 0]}>
            <InteractiveLever onSuccess={() => setSuccess(true)} />
            {/* Ground */}
            <RigidBody type="fixed">
              <mesh position={[0, -2, 0]} rotation={[-Math.PI / 2, 0, 0]}>
                <planeGeometry args={[50, 50]} />
                <meshStandardMaterial color="#4ade80" />
              </mesh>
            </RigidBody>
          </Physics>
        </Canvas>
      </div>

      {/* FOREGROUND: UI & Character (The Kinship Layer) */}
      <div className="absolute bottom-10 left-10 z-10 flex items-end gap-4">
        
        {/* The Animated Guide - Reacts to the 3D World */}
        <div className="bg-white/90 p-2 rounded-full shadow-xl backdrop-blur-sm border-4 border-yellow-400">
          <Assistant mood={success ? 100 : 0} />
        </div>

        {/* The Dialog Box - Bouncy animation via Framer Motion */}
        <motion.div 
          initial={{ scale: 0, opacity: 0 }}
          animate={{ scale: 1, opacity: 1 }}
          className="bg-white p-6 rounded-2xl shadow-lg max-w-md border-b-4 border-gray-200"
        >
          <h2 className="text-xl font-bold text-gray-800">
            {success ? "Great Job! 🎉" : "The rock is too heavy..."}
          </h2>
          <p className="text-gray-600 mt-2">
            {success 
              ? "You used leverage to multiply your force!" 
              : "Try moving the fulcrum (the triangle) closer to the rock."}
          </p>
        </motion.div>
      </div>
      
    </div>
  );
}
```

### Summary of the "Kinship" Stack

1. **React Three Fiber:** Makes the world feel real (shadows, lighting) rather than a flat diagram.
    
2. **Rive:** Gives the app a "soul" via a reactive character.
    
3. **Framer Motion:** Makes the UI feel like a toy (bouncy, fluid) rather than a tool.
    

This combination allows you to teach "Expressive Truths" (Art) and "Functional Truths" (STEM) in an environment that feels like a high-quality video game, which is the native language of the children you are teaching.


```
import React, { useState, useEffect } from 'react';
import { Canvas } from '@react-three/fiber';
import { Physics, RigidBody } from '@react-three/rapier';
import { useRive, useStateMachineInput } from '@rive-app/react-canvas';

    src: 'assistant_character.riv',
    stateMachines: 'State Machine 1',
    autoplay: true,
  });

  const moodInput = useStateMachineInput(rive, 'State Machine 1', 'MoodLevel');
  
  useEffect(() => {
    if (moodInput) moodInput.value = mood; // 0 = Sad, 100 = Happy
  }, [mood, moodInput]);

  return <RiveComponent style={{ width: '150px', height: '150px' }} />;
};

  // Use Rapier for real physics interaction
  return (
    <RigidBody colliders="hull" onCollisionEnter={onSuccess}>
      <mesh castShadow receiveShadow>

        <meshStandardMaterial color="#8B4513" />
      </mesh>

  return (
    <div className="h-screen w-screen bg-blue-50 relative overflow-hidden">

          <ambientLight intensity={0.5} />
          <directionalLight position={[10, 10, 5]} castShadow />
          
          <Physics gravity={[0, -9.81, 0]}>
            <InteractiveLever onSuccess={() => setSuccess(true)} />
            {/* Ground */}
            <RigidBody type="fixed">
              <mesh position={[0, -2, 0]} rotation={[-Math.PI / 2, 0, 0]}>
                <planeGeometry args={[50, 50]} />
                <meshStandardMaterial color="#4ade80" />
              </mesh>
            </RigidBody>

        </Canvas>
      </div>

      {/* FOREGROUND: UI & Character (The Kinship Layer) */}
      <div className="absolute bottom-10 left-10 z-10 flex items-end gap-4">
        
        {/* The Animated Guide - Reacts to the 3D World */}
        <div className="bg-white/90 p-2 rounded-full shadow-xl backdrop-blur-sm border-4 border-yellow-400">
          <Assistant mood={success ? 100 : 0} />
        </div>

        {/* The Dialog Box - Bouncy animation via Framer Motion */}
        <motion.div 
          initial={{ scale: 0, opacity: 0 }}
          animate={{ scale: 1, opacity: 1 }}
          className="bg-white p-6 rounded-2xl shadow-lg max-w-md border-b-4 border-gray-200"
        >
          <h2 className="text-xl font-bold text-gray-800">
            {success ? "Great Job! 🎉" : "The rock is too heavy..."}
          </h2>
          <p className="text-gray-600 mt-2">
            {success 
              ? "You used leverage to multiply your force!" 
              : "Try moving the fulcrum (the triangle) closer to the rock."}
          </p>
        </motion.div>
      </div>
      
    </div>
  );
}Here is how you structure a component that combines **R3F (3D)** with **Rive (Character)** to create that friendly "Kinship" feel.

## Supporting Pages

### **Geometric Evolution Timeline**
**File**: `<!DOCTYPE html> <html lang="en"> <head> .html`
**Purpose**: 6-slide journey through geometric history
**Color Scheme**: **Dark background (#0a0a0a, #1a1a2e) + Gold accents**

**Slides**:
1. **1D Basic** - Points, lines, tallies (70,000 BCE - 2560 BCE)
2. **1D→2D** - Spirals, meanders, hieroglyphs (6000 BCE - 500 CE)
3. **2D Systems** - Grids, tessellations, Islamic patterns (1400 BCE - 1350 CE)
4. **3D Linear** - Columns, arches, buttresses (3000 BCE - 1937 CE)
5. **2D on 3D** - Pottery decoration, dome patterns (530 BCE - present)
6. **Full 3D** - Pyramids, domes, parametric architecture (2560 BCE - 21st century)

**Navigation**: Keyboard arrows, swipe gestures, clickable tabs
**Image Cards**:
```css
Background: rgba(255,255,255,0.05)
Border: 1px solid rgba(255,215,0,0.2)
Hover Border: gold
Image Glow: 0 10px 30px rgba(255,215,0,0.2)
```

**Pedagogical Tags**:
- Culture icons (🏛️ Greek, 🐉 Chinese, 🕌 Islamic)
- Date periods (📅 3500 BCE, 1200 CE, etc.)
- Math concepts (📐 Circumference, Ï€, Golden ratio)
- Grade levels (K-2, 3-5, 6-8)

## API Endpoints (Backend)

### Lesson Management
- `GET /api/lessons/library?grade=3` - Load lessons from CSV
- Returns: 50 lessons with metadata (deity, element, sections)

### Manifest System
- `GET /api/manifests?grade=3` - List all verified manifests
- `GET /api/manifests/:filename` - Get manifest with parsed YAML + images
- Returns: Metadata, British/Met Museum URLs, lesson sections

### Research Integration
- `POST /api/research/execute` - Perplexity AI research
- Params: `{ prompt: "your query" }`
- Returns: Content + citations

### Museum Search
- 
- `GET /api/museums/search?query=shamash` - Search Met + British Museum
- Returns: Artifact images with metadata

2. Query: "Shamash solar disk symbolism in Mesopotamia"
3. Receive AI-powered research with citations
4. Cross-reference with Museum APIs
5. Export findings for documentation

- **Color-coded sections** = Day A (blue), Day B (orange), Art (purple)
- **Glowing effects** = Interactive elements (buttons, cards)

### Interaction Patterns
- **Click lessons** → Full detail view
- **Hover cards** → Gold border + glow effect
- **Back button** → Return to browsing
- **Smooth scrolling** → Auto-scroll to top on detail view

### Trustworthiness
- ✅ Museum APIs (British Museum, Met Museum)
- ✅ Batch downloaders with verified URLs
- ✅ Complete manifests with metadata
- ❌ Curator database (unreliable image names)

## Tagging System (Auto-Ingestion)

### **Automatic Tag Extraction**
Every lesson and manifest is automatically tagged upon ingestion with comprehensive metadata:

### Tag Categories

#### 1. **Grade Level**
- `Grade 3`, `Grade 4`, `Grade 5`
- **Source**: Extracted from `Lesson_Code` (e.g., G3-1A)

#### 2. **Geometric Element**
- `Circle`, `Crescent`, `Star`, `Triangle`, `Square`, `Rectangle`, `Hexagon`, `Octagon`
- **Source**: `Geometric_Element` field
- **Display Color**: Gold

#### 3. **Deity**
- `Shamash`, `Sin`, `Ishtar`, `Ninurta`, `Enki`, `Enlil`, `Inanna`
- **Source**: `Myth/Deity` field
- **Display Color**: Gold

#### 4. **Learning Modes**
- `Metaphor` (Day A) - **Color**: Blue (#4fc3f7)
- `Function` (Day B) - **Color**: Orange (#ff9800)
- `Art Activity` - **Color**: Purple (#ce93d8)
- `Invention` - **Color**: Green (#81c784)
- `Project` - **Color**: Bronze (#CD7F32)

#### 5. **Skills & Standards**
- `SEL` (Social-Emotional Learning) - **Color**: Purple
- `Visual Semiotics` - **Color**: Blue
- `STEM History` - **Color**: Green
- `Mathematical Properties` - **Color**: Green
- `Material Culture` - **Color**: Bronze

#### 6. **Topics** (Keyword-Based Extraction)
Auto-detected from metaphor/function descriptions:
- `Justice` - Keywords: justice, fairness, fair, equal
- `Cycles` - Keywords: cycle, renewal, time, growth
- `Complexity` - Keywords: complex, pattern, system
- `Stability` - Keywords: stable, strong, support, balance
- `Protection` - Keywords: protect, shield, defense

#### 7. **Curriculum Structure**
- `Week 1` through `Week 16`
- `Day A`, `Day B`, `Bridge`
- Section codes: `A1-A10`, `B1-B7`

#### 8. **Source Verification**
- `CSV Data` - From curriculum spreadsheet
- `Verified Manifest` - From complete lesson manifests
- `British Museum` - Verified museum API
- `Met Museum` - Verified museum API
- `Batch Downloader` - From verified image scripts
- `Museum Sourced` - General museum verification

#### 9. **Historical Period**
- `Mesopotamian`, `Ancient`, `Classical`, `Medieval`, `Renaissance`, `Modern`

### Tag Display System

**On Lesson Cards**:
- Shows first 8 tags in color-coded pills
- "+X more" indicator for additional tags
- Tags appear at bottom of card with border separator
- Color-coded by category:
  - **Blue**: Metaphor, Visual Semiotics
  - **Orange**: Function
  - **Purple**: Art Activity, SEL
  - **Green**: Invention, STEM History
  - **Bronze**: Project
  - **Gold**: Element, Deity, Grade, Week

**Tag Pill Style**:
```css
background: rgba(255,255,255,0.1)
border: 1px solid [category-color]
color: [category-color]
padding: 3px 10px
border-radius: 12px
font-size: 0.75rem
```

### Automatic Tagging Functions

#### `extractTags(lesson, source)`
Extracts all tags from a CSV lesson:
```javascript
{
  content_type: 'Lesson',
  source: 'CSV Data',
  tags: ['Grade 3', 'Week 1', 'Circle', 'Shamash', 'Metaphor', 'Function', ...],
  metadata: {
    grade: 3,
    week: 1,
    element: 'Circle',
    deity: 'Shamash',
    section: 'A1',
    day: 'A'
  }
}
```

#### `extractManifestTags(manifest)`
Extracts tags from verified manifests:
```javascript
{
  content_type: 'Verified Manifest',
  source: 'Verified Manifest',
  tags: ['Grade 3', 'Circle', 'Shamash', 'Perfection and justice', 'British Museum', 'Met Museum', 'Verified Content'],
  metadata: {
    lesson_code: 'G3-1A/B',
    grade: 3,
    deity: 'Shamash',
    element: 'circle',
    metaphor: 'perfection_and_justice',
    primary_innovation: 'potter's_wheel'
  }
}
```

### Tag Usage

**Search Enhancement**:
- Tags make lessons searchable by any metadata field
- Filter by clicking tag pills (future feature)
- Multi-tag filtering for precise results

**Organization**:
- Group lessons by element, deity, or skill
- Track curriculum coverage (which weeks/topics covered)
- Identify skill gaps (which SEL topics need more lessons)

**Parent Presentation**:
- Quickly show "all Metaphor lessons" or "all Art Activity lessons"
- Display skill coverage (SEL, Visual Semiotics, STEM)
- Demonstrate curriculum comprehensiveness

**Future Enhancements**:

Got you — and your “ramble” actually nails the **real requirement**: Content+Images isn’t just a lesson-building convenience… it’s **research instrumentation** for testing hypotheses about symbol choices → functions → math outcomes (place value / zero), with **efficiency** and **time** as analyzable concepts. 🧠⚙️⏳

# Spec: Content + Images v1 (Graph-Native)

## 0) Non-Negotiables

- **Every image becomes a graph node** (or attaches to one) and is linkable to:
    
    - Lesson SSOT
        
    - Civilization
        
    - Artifact
        
    - Symbol
        
    - Function
        
    - Time period / date range
        
    - Concepts like **efficiency**
        
- **Image “object” ≠ placement** (one image, many placements)
    
- **Auto-retagging** exists (tags update when new truth is known)
    
- **Visibility controls** exist (hide / teacher-only / remove-from-student-output)
    
- **Presets** exist (saved graph views and filters to repeat analyses)
    

---

# 1) UI Views (Flexible “Image Page” Modes)

### View A — Lesson Image Registrar (must-have)

Purpose: “Show me every image used in this lesson” (and where)

Capabilities:

- Group by **section** (A1…B8)
    
- Show **usage count** (placements)
    
- Jump-to-placement (click → scroll + highlight anchor)
    
- “Missing metadata” warnings:
    
    - missing credit/rights
        
    - missing tags (symbol/function/time)
        
    - missing caption
        
- Export: “Credits / Sources page” (teacher-facing)
    

### View B — Curate Inbox (must-have)

Purpose: triage new images before they enter lessons/graph

Capabilities:

- Filter: source, rights, resolution, duplicates, tag completeness
    
- Batch tag + batch attach to node(s)
    
- “Suggested tags” panel (auto)
    
- “Attach to spine” action (Lesson SSOT / Civilization / Artifact)
    

### View C — Moodboard / Board View (strongly recommended)

Purpose: pattern spotting + symbol comparison (your research angle)

Capabilities:

- Drag images into clusters (“Symbol variants,” “Same function, different form,” etc.)
    
- Save clusters as:
    
    - a **gallery object**
        
    - a **hypothesis set**
        
    - a **preset**
        
- Quick compare within a cluster
    

### View D — Compare View (must-have for your theory)

Purpose: minor symbol differences, side-by-side inspection

Capabilities:

- 2-up / 4-up compare
    
- Lock zoom/pan
    
- Show metadata overlay:
    
    - symbol tag
        
    - function tag
        
    - time range
        
    - source
        
- “Promote winner” (mark preferred for placement)
    

### View E — Placement Map (recommended)

Purpose: “Where do visuals exist / where are gaps?”

Capabilities:

- Lesson outline with image markers per section
    
- “Gaps” suggestions (“B3 has no visuals”)
    
- Heatmap option (dense vs sparse)
    

---

# 2) Text ↔ Image Interactions (Footnotes + Append)

### A) Highlight → Footnote with Image (must-have)

User flow:

1. Highlight text in editor
    
2. Click “Attach image”
    
3. Choose image(s)
    
4. Choose display style:
    
    - hover preview
        
    - inline thumbnail
        
    - margin callout
        
    - footnote marker that opens a mini-gallery
        

Storage rule (important):

- Don’t store fragile character offsets.
    
- Store **anchor strategies**:
    
    - `block_id` (best if you use block-based editor)
        
    - else: `quote_snippet + paragraph_index + occurrence`
        
    - plus fallback matching
        

### B) “Select a few → append to correct location” (must-have)

Three placement modes:

- **Manual:** drag image onto block/paragraph
    
- **Assisted:** system suggests sections/anchors based on tags + keywords + mentions
    
- **Registrar-only:** add to lesson registry now, place later
    

### C) Narrowed search → “Apply set to section” (must-have)

- If user filtered down to 10 images and selects 3:
    
    - one click: “Attach to A4 Artifact Encounter”
        
    - optional: “Attach as gallery” vs “Place individually”
        

---

# 3) Auto-Retagging System (Your “Retag Itself” Requirement)

## A) Tag Inheritance Rules (must-have)

When you know values, propagate them.

Examples:

- If a lesson is tagged:
    
    - `civilization: Mesopotamia`, `week: XII`, `element: circle`  
        then new placements inherit those unless overridden.
        
- If an image is attached to Artifact X, inherit:
    
    - civilization
        
    - likely time range
        
    - material/medium tags (if stored on artifact node)
        

## B) Tag Suggestions (must-have)

When importing/curating:

- Suggest:
    
    - symbol candidates (circle, star, crescent, wedge)
        
    - function candidates (rotation, measurement, counting, logistics)
        
    - concept candidates (efficiency, standardization, compression)
        
    - time range candidates (from source metadata)
        

## C) Auto-Retagging Trigger Events (must-have)

Retag runs when:

- image is linked to a new node
    
- artifact metadata updated
    
- timeline range corrected
    
- user promotes a tag to “truth”
    

## D) Conflict Handling (must-have)

- Tags have **confidence** + **provenance**:
    
    - `user_confirmed`
        
    - `museum_metadata`
        
    - `ai_suggested`
        
- UI shows conflicts (“time range differs across sources”)
    
- User can pick:
    
    - override
        
    - keep both with confidence levels
        

---

# 4) Hide / Remove / Output Rules (must-have)

Images need lifecycle states:

### Image visibility flags

- `visible_in_student` (default false until approved)
    
- `visible_in_teacher`
    
- `hidden` (still in system, not shown in UI by default)
    
- `archived` (kept for history)
    
- `deleted` (soft-delete only unless you truly want removal)
    

### Placement-level visibility

Even if image is generally visible, a placement can be:

- teacher-only
    
- hidden in exported textbook but shown in research view
    

This is how you keep your research corpus “complete” while outputs stay clean.

---

# 5) Where to Save Content + Images (v1 that can scale)

## Best-practice split

- **Database** stores: lesson text, image metadata, placements, tags, graph relationships
    
- **File storage** stores: actual image files + thumbnails
    

### Option 1: Local-first (fastest now)

- Images: local drive folder structure
    
    - `/assets/images/originals/`
        
    - `/assets/images/medium/`
        
    - `/assets/images/thumbs/`
        
- DB: SQLite (or JSON + later migrate)
    

### Option 2: Team/Cloud-ready

- Images: Supabase Storage (or S3)
    
- DB: Postgres (Supabase)
    

✅ Either way: DB stores **paths/URLs**, not the binary image in the DB.

---

# 6) Graph Integration (Bare Spine + Nodes)

You said:

> “potentially stand around as nodes connected to a bare spine — Lesson SSOT — Civilization.”

So define a **spine** like:

- Civilization → Timeline → Week → Lesson SSOT → Section  
    And everything else orbits it:
    
- Image, Artifact, Symbol, Function, Concept (Efficiency), Standard
    

## Required node types for v1

- `LessonSSOT`
    
- `Civilization`
    
- `TimeRange` (or `Period`)
    
- `Artifact`
    
- `Image`
    
- `Symbol`
    
- `Function`
    
- `Concept` (including Efficiency)
    
- `Placement` (optional node; can also be an edge with properties)
    

## Required edge types

- `DEPicts` (Image → Symbol)
    
- `FROM_SOURCE` (Image → Artifact/Museum Record)
    
- `USED_IN` (Image → LessonSSOT)
    
- `PLACED_AT` (Image → Section with anchor props)
    
- `ASSOCIATED_WITH` (Artifact ↔ Civilization)
    
- `OCCURS_IN_TIME` (anything → TimeRange)
    
- `HAS_FUNCTION` (Symbol/Artifact → Function)
    
- `RELATES_TO_CONCEPT` (Symbol/Function/Artifact → Efficiency)
    

---

# 7) Presets (Critical for Your Research)

Presets are saved “investigation modes”:

### Must-have preset types

- **Lens preset**: (Artifacts view, filter: symbol=circle, time=Uruk)
    
- **Hypothesis preset**: a saved cluster + query (“symbol variance vs function variance”)
    
- **Lesson build preset**: “Day B images only,” “assessment-only,” etc.
    

### Preset stores

- camera position / graph view state (if 3D)
    
- active filters
    
- active lens
    
- pinned nodes
    
- open windows
    

---

# 8) What kind of graph node can deduce functions + statistically significant relationships?

This is the science-y part. You’ll want a **hybrid approach**:

## A) Graph database / knowledge graph for structure

Use it to represent:

- entities + relationships + time ranges + tags
    
- fast querying (“show all images with symbol X during period Y linked to functions Z”)
    

Good for:

- retrieval
    
- pattern surfacing
    
- building your presets
    

## B) Analytical layer for statistics + inference

A graph _by itself_ doesn’t “prove” significance.  
You compute significance by turning parts of the graph into **tables/features**, then running tests.

### What you’ll eventually compute

- frequency of symbol variants per time window
    
- co-occurrence strength (e.g., circle + function: rotation/measurement)
    
- trend changes over time (symbol shift before/after a period)
    
- clustering (do symbol sets cluster with administrative efficiency artifacts?)
    
- correlation with “efficiency” proxy metrics (see below)
    

### Tools / methods (conceptually)

- association tests (chi-square / Fisher exact)
    
- regression models (predict function from symbol + time + civ)
    
- graph embeddings (node2vec / GraphSAGE) to find “similar” nodes
    
- community detection (Louvain / Leiden) for emergent clusters
    

## C) Define “Efficiency” and “Time” as first-class citizens (your insight is key)

To study efficiency, you need:

- a timeline representation (ranges + uncertainty)
    
- proxies for efficiency (since it’s abstract)
    

**Efficiency proxies you can encode:**

- compression/standardization evidence (e.g., accounting tablets, standardized measures)
    
- transaction density / record-keeping complexity (counts of administrative artifacts)
    
- distribution networks indicated by artifacts (trade seals, weights)
    
- repeated standardized symbol usage across contexts
    

Even if the theory is wrong, this setup lets you _test it_ instead of guessing.

# 9) Content+Images v1 Acceptance Criteria (so you know it’s “done”)

You can:

- open a lesson section
    
- highlight a paragraph and attach 1–3 images as footnotes
    
- see those images appear in:
    
    - registrar view
        
    - placement map
        
    - graph as nodes linked to lesson/civilization/time/symbol
        
- bulk-select images and append them to a section (registrar-first or placed)
    
- change a tag (e.g., correct time range) and watch related images **retag/update views**
    
- hide images from student output while keeping them in research graph

# # Designing a knowledge system for detecting learning phase transitions with permanent intellectual traceability

The theoretical foundations for this knowledge system exist across cognitive science, educational assessment, and systems architecture—but no integrated implementation currently combines them. **Knowledge Space Theory provides the mathematical backbone for phase transition detection**, event sourcing architectures enable permanent traceability, and **parallel constraint satisfaction models offer the best existing framework for your "cat's cradle" cognitive topology**. What follows synthesizes research across six domains into an actionable architectural vision.

The core insight from this research: learning phase transitions are observable through specific markers (increased variability preceding transition, bimodality during transition, sudden jumps, hysteresis), and these can be computationally detected using the mathematical structures already proven in systems like ALEKS. Meanwhile, permanence requires treating intellectual work not as mutable state but as an immutable event stream with cryptographic verification—a pattern well-established in event sourcing and append-only database architectures.

## Phase transitions have observable markers that systems can detect

Dynamic systems theory, catastrophe theory, and skill acquisition research converge on a crucial finding: **learning doesn't accumulate linearly but reorganizes through qualitative phase transitions**. Thelen and Smith's work establishes that development emerges from self-organizing systems where small quantitative changes trigger qualitative reorganization of attractor landscapes—the moment when accumulated knowledge "clicks" into ability.

Van der Maas and Molenaar's cusp catastrophe model specifies six testable **"catastrophe flags"** marking genuine phase transitions: bimodality (scores cluster in two peaks rather than normal distribution), sudden jumps (discontinuous change with continuous input), hysteresis (path-dependence—going up differs from going down), divergence, anomalous variance (increased variability near transition points), and inaccessibility of intermediate states. These provide computationally detectable signatures.

The Dreyfus skill acquisition model maps the full progression your system would track:

|Stage|Knowledge Type|Observable Marker|
|---|---|---|
|**Knowledge**|Explicit/declarative|Can state facts; concepts in knowledge state|
|**Ability**|Emerging procedural|Can perform in context; new attractor stabilizes|
|**Skill**|Implicit dominant|Intuitive, automatic; performance under stress|
|**Asset** (generative capacity)|Embodied/transformative|Extends domain; creates new possibilities|

Knowledge Space Theory (Falmagne and Doignon) provides the mathematical machinery. In ALEKS, **24-30 problems can pinpoint a student's state among 57,000+ possibilities** because correct responses imply mastery of all prerequisite concepts—evidence propagates through the knowledge structure. The "inner fringe" and "outer fringe" concepts precisely capture the boundary between current capability and ready-to-learn zones, making phase transition detection computationally tractable.

## Permanence requires treating all changes as immutable events

For a system where intellectual work cannot be quietly erased or replaced, the architecture must embrace **append-only storage with cryptographic verification**. Event sourcing—storing the sequence of all changes rather than current state—makes deletions impossible by design. Every fragment, draft, connection, and evolution becomes a permanent, timestamped event.

Three database technologies support this requirement:

**XTDB** (formerly Crux) provides bitemporal SQL with two timelines: `SYSTEM_TIME` (immutable append-only record) and `VALID_TIME` (business logic). System time cannot be modified—only appended—creating tamper-evident audit trails. Query any past state: `SELECT * FROM ideas FOR SYSTEM_TIME AS OF '2024-01-01'`.

**Datomic** stores all data as immutable "datoms" (entity, attribute, value, time). The database itself is a value—previous states remain queryable forever. Updates add new facts; old facts persist. Every transaction becomes a traceable entity with built-in attribution.

**EventStoreDB** is purpose-built for event sourcing with an append-only event log, native pub/sub, and proven scalability to billions of events. The data model directly supports tracking idea evolution: `IdeaCreated → DraftStarted → LinkedTo → Revised → Published`.

For cryptographic proof of existence, **blockchain timestamping** anchors document hashes in Bitcoin transactions, creating permanent proof that content existed at a specific time. Services like OriginStamp batch hashes cost-effectively. This creates legally defensible provenance already accepted as evidence in Italy, China, and some US jurisdictions.

The recommended stack combines local immutable storage (XTDB/Datomic) + Git-style content-addressable hashing (Merkle trees) + periodic blockchain timestamping for external verification. **The combination provides defense-in-depth: even if one layer fails, others prove authenticity.**

## The "cat's cradle" finds its closest match in parallel constraint satisfaction

Your vision of cognitive domains as mutually supporting and constraining—a tensegrity structure where each element both enables and limits others—matches **Smolensky's Harmony Theory** more closely than any other existing framework.

Harmony Theory describes cognition as distributed across interconnected processing units settling into states of "maximum Harmony" through parallel constraint satisfaction. Key properties include:

- **Mutual constraint**: Each cognitive element constrains and is constrained by others
- **Dynamic equilibrium**: Systems settle into optimal states through parallel interactions
- **Emergent structure**: Apparent hierarchies arise from flat, non-hierarchical architectures

O'Connor, Cree, and McRae's flat attractor network research demonstrates empirically that **conceptual hierarchies can emerge from networks with no built-in hierarchical structure**—hierarchy is an emergent property of mutual constraint satisfaction rather than predetermined architecture. This directly supports your intuition that domains form a tension structure where relationships are defined by mutual support rather than sequence.

Gibson's ecological psychology adds the **mutuality principle**: organism and environment are two parts of a whole system, each implying the other. Affordances—neither purely objective nor subjective—cut across the dichotomy. This provides theoretical grounding for defining cognitive capacities through their relationships rather than in isolation.

Category theory offers mathematical formalization through **functors** (structure-preserving maps), **natural transformations** (relationships between functors), and **adjunctions** (formal means for capturing "natural" relationships). Phillips and Wilson argue category theory provides necessary and sufficient conditions for cognitive systematicity without requiring hierarchical structure.

## Standard relationships map to established classification schemes

Knowledge Space Theory's **surmise relations** directly formalize your "necessary" relationship: if mastering concept B requires knowing concept A, any knowledge state containing B must also contain A. This is computationally implemented in ALEKS as precedence diagrams.

The four-way classification you propose maps onto existing frameworks:

|Your Category|KST Equivalent|Bayesian Network|Semantic Relation|
|---|---|---|---|
|**Necessary** (must know A before B)|Surmise relation|Directed prerequisite edge|requires/isRequiredBy|
|**Sufficient** (A is enough for B)|Rarely modeled; addressed via multiple paths to same state|Conditional probability = 1|implies|
|**Related** (share something, neither causes other)|Co-occurrence patterns|Undirected edges|references/isPartOf|
|**Unrelated** (no connection)|No path between concepts|Conditionally independent|—|

A significant gap in the literature: **"sufficient" conditions are rarely modeled explicitly**. Most frameworks emphasize necessary conditions (prerequisites) rather than sufficient conditions. Your system could contribute novel theoretical work by formalizing sufficiency in learning—when knowing X is actually enough for Y, not merely correlated with it.

Cognitive Diagnostic Models using Q-matrices can model complex combinations: DINA requires mastery of ALL attributes (conjunctive), DINO requires ANY ONE (disjunctive), and G-DINA provides weighted combinations. These could formalize your four relationship types within a probabilistic inference framework.

## Heat mapping propagates evidence through knowledge topology

Your "radius of implication" concept has direct implementation in Knowledge Space Theory's **layer structure**. Research on ALEKS shows that correct response rates follow a gradient from outer layers (near zero) to inner layers (~0.27) to deep inner layers (higher retention). **Reliable inference extends approximately 1-2 hops from assessed concepts**, with reliability decreasing at greater graph distance.

Structure-based Knowledge Tracing (SKT) explicitly models influence propagation through:

- **Synchronization**: For undirected relations—updates spread equally
- **Partial propagation**: For directed relations—updates flow along edges with decay

Graph-based Knowledge Tracing reformulates assessment as **time-series node-level classification on a graph neural network**. Knowledge concepts are nodes, relations are edges, and embeddings propagate information across the structure. This is the closest existing implementation to your "heat map" vision.

Belief propagation provides the mathematical foundation. Each node sends messages encoding beliefs about local state; nodes update based on incoming messages; the process iterates until convergence. Evidence at one node propagates through the graph with **influence decaying based on distance and edge weights**—exactly the "radius implication" behavior you described.

For visualization, medical education's **milestone competency heat maps** provide the clearest existing model: rows = competencies, columns = learners, cells = cumulative achievement, with color gradients indicating developmental progression. This approach could directly inform your system's visualization layer.

## Theory bootstrapping through dialogue has rigorous methodology

The rigid separation of "context of discovery" from "context of justification" has collapsed. Kuhn argued that discovery processes follow identifiable patterns; the creative and evaluative phases intertwine. This validates **structured dialogue as an epistemologically legitimate method** for theory formation.

Grounded theory's **constant comparative method** provides the core procedure: compare data with data, codes with codes, codes with categories; continue until theoretical saturation. Every dialogue exchange surfaces data points that must be compared with previous ones, progressively refining emergent understanding.

Polanyi established that **"we can know more than we can tell"**—experts cannot fully articulate their tacit knowledge. But Nonaka and Takeuchi's SECI model identifies **externalization** (tacit → explicit through metaphor, analogy, dialogue) as a distinct knowledge conversion mode. The realistic position: externalization is partial and contextual, not complete conversion. This means theory articulation is always incomplete, and dialogue must continue.

For AI as Socratic interlocutor, research shows effectiveness requires **configuring AI as critical questioner, not agreeable assistant**. Key techniques:

- One question at a time
- Clarification before critique
- Balance challenge with productive direction
- Document emerging theoretical elements

Princeton NLP's Socratic Method for LLMs demonstrates that multiple AI agents engaging in dialogue can **"self-discover knowledge and expand perspectives."** However, hybrid approaches with human oversight outperform AI-only sessions—human-AI co-tutoring raised critical thinking scores 23% over AI-only in one pilot study.

## Architectural synthesis: connecting theory to implementation

A system implementing these requirements would have the following components:

### Data layer

- **XTDB or Datomic** as primary datastore for immutable, bitemporal storage
- **Git-style content-addressable storage** for knowledge artifacts
- **Knowledge graph** (Neo4j or similar) for representing domain topology
- **Periodic blockchain anchoring** for external proof of existence

### Assessment engine

- **Knowledge Space Theory implementation** using the open-source kst/kstMatrix libraries
- **Belief propagation** for radius implication across knowledge graph
- **Phase transition detection** monitoring catastrophe flags (variance, bimodality, sudden jumps)
- **Layer tracking** for heat map visualization

### Cognitive domain model

- **Parallel constraint satisfaction network** implementing Harmony Theory principles
- **Dynamic equilibrium computation** for mutual support/constraint relationships
- **Emergent hierarchy detection** rather than predetermined structure

### Dialogue system

- **Structured Socratic prompting** configured for theory bootstrapping
- **Constant comparison tracking** across dialogue sessions
- **Theoretical memo generation** documenting emerging insights
- **Saturation detection** to identify when dialogue stops yielding new understanding

### Traceability system

- **Event sourcing** for all intellectual work changes
- **Cryptographic hash chains** linking all revisions
- **Attribution metadata** on every event
- **Temporal queries** enabling "what did we understand at time T"

## The system you're designing addresses a genuine gap

No existing system integrates phase transition detection with permanent traceability of intellectual work. ALEKS implements Knowledge Space Theory rigorously but doesn't track the evolution of understanding over time with immutable audit trails. Knowledge management tools like Obsidian offer version history but lack cognitive diagnostic capabilities. The frameworks exist; the integration doesn't.

Your "cat's cradle" cognitive topology represents original theoretical contribution. While parallel constraint satisfaction, ecological mutuality, and dynamic systems theory provide foundations, no existing framework explicitly uses **tensegrity as an organizing principle** combining structural integrity from balanced tensions, mutual support and constraint as defining relationships, dynamic equilibrium rather than static hierarchy, and context-dependent emergence of functional organization.

The permanence requirement addresses a real and documented problem—systematic erasure of intellectual work. **Event sourcing with cryptographic verification creates a system where this erasure becomes technically impossible**, not merely policy-prohibited. Every fragment, every draft, every connection persists with timestamped attribution.

The phase transition framing shifts assessment from measuring accumulation to **detecting qualitative reorganization**—a fundamentally different epistemology of learning. The theoretical foundations are solid (Dynamic Systems Theory, catastrophe theory, embodied cognition), the mathematical machinery exists (Knowledge Space Theory, belief propagation), and the observable markers are well-characterized. What's needed is implementation that treats these phase transitions as the primary phenomenon to detect rather than side effects to ignore.
